Local Well-Posedness of the Motion of Inviscid Liquid Crystals with a Free Surface Boundary
Chenyun Luo and Hang Yu

Abstract
In this article, we prove the local well-posedness of the free-boundary Lin–Liu equations describing the motion of inviscid nematic liquid crystals in the presence of surface tension in Lagrangian coordinates. It is well known that a priori energy estimates alone are insufficient for establishing local existence in free-boundary problems involving inviscid fluid equations, primarily due to the loss of symmetry in the linearized equations. The main challenge is to develop an effective approximate system of equations that is asymptotically consistent with the free-boundary Lin–Liu model expressed in the Lagrangian coordinates. This system must accurately capture the coupling between the fluid motion and the harmonic heat flows within the interior, as well as the regularity of the moving boundary.



1
Introduction

We study the motion of inviscid nematic liquid crystals occupying a moving domain
\mathcal{D}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
described by the simplified Ericksen–Leslie equations (also known as Lin–Liu equations):

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+(u\cdot\nabla)u+\nabla P=-\nabla\cdot(\nabla d\odot\nabla d)\quad\text{in $\mathcal{D}$,}\\
\nabla\cdot u=0\quad\text{in $\mathcal{D}$,}\\
\partial_{t}d+(u\cdot\nabla)d-\Delta d=d|\nabla d|^{2}\quad\text{in $\mathcal{D}$,}\end{cases}

(1.1)

Here,
\mathcal{D}=\cup_{0\leq t\leq T}\{t\}\times\mathcal{D}_{t}
,
u:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the velocity field,
d:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
is the unit orientation vector fields of nematic liquid crystals, where
\nabla d\cdot\nabla d
is a
2
-tensor with components
(\nabla d\odot\nabla d)_{ij}:=\nabla_{i}d\cdot\nabla_{j}d
, and
P:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
is the pressure.
We equip (
1.1
) the initial conditions:

\mathcal{D}_{t}|_{t=0}=\mathcal{D}_{0},\quad u(0,\cdot)=u_{0},\quad\text{and }\quad d(0,\cdot)=d_{0},

(1.2)

as well as the boundary conditions:

\begin{cases}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla)|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}\in\mathcal{T}(\partial\mathcal{D}),\\
P=\sigma H\quad\text{on $\partial\mathcal{D}$},\\
\nabla_{n}d=0\quad\text{on $\partial\mathcal{D}$},\end{cases}

(1.3)

where
\partial\mathcal{D}=\cup_{0\leq t\leq T}\{t\}\times\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}
.
In (
1.3
),
\mathcal{T}(\partial\mathcal{D})
is the tangent bundle of
\partial\mathcal{D}
, and
n
is the outward-pointing unit normal of
\partial\mathcal{D}
. Also, we denote by
H
the twice of mean curvature of the moving boundary, and by
\sigma>0
the surface tension coefficient.

For the sake of simplicity and clean notations, we will only consider the case

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(0,1)

(1.4)

to avoid using local coordinate charts in a more general case. We will use
\partial\mathcal{D}^{+}(t)
to represent the top moving boundary, and
\partial\mathcal{D}^{-}(t)=\{x_{3}=0\}
to denote the fixed bottom. In other words,
\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}=\partial\mathcal{D}^{+}(t)\cup\partial\mathcal{D}^{-}(t)
.
Additionally, we prescribe the natural slip boundary condition of the velocity on the fixed bottom, i.e.,

\displaystyle u_{3}=0,\quad\text{on }\partial\mathcal{D}^{-}(t).

(1.5)

1.1
History and background

Free boundary problems in fluid mechanics, nematic liquid crystals, and harmonic heat flow have been extensively studied over the past decades. Here, we mention only a few works relevant to our problem.

The system (
1.1
) was first introduced by Lin in
[
54
,
51
]
as a simplified version of the Ericksen-Leslie model proposed by Ericksen
[
18
]
and Leslie
[
47
]
, which mathematically retains some basic nonlinearity structures. In
[
52
,
50
,
53
]
, Lin and Liu initiated the mathematical analysis of the system which established the global existence of the approximate solution in dimensions
2
and
3
. Since then, many research works have been established on its well-posedness, see
[
29
,
32
,
31
,
33
,
45
,
49
,
56
,
58
,
70
,
72
,
79
]
and the references therein. We also refer to
[
57
]
for a survey of the mathematical developments in the theory of liquid crystals. Readers may also consult
[
30
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
34
]
,
[
44
]
, and
[
55
]
for results concerning singularities and blow-up in related topics.

In addition, free-boundary problems in fluid mechanics have been extensively studied over the past few decades. For the incompressible Euler equation, earlier works mainly focused on irrotational fluids, also known as the water wave problem. In two dimensions, Nalimov
[
63
]
was the first to establish local well-posedness without surface tension for small initial data. See also Craig
[
12
]
and Yosihara
[
80
]
. Under the Talor-sign condition, in dimensions
2
and
3
, Wu
[
75
,
76
]
developed the local existence for general initial data. The Taylor-sign condition is crucial in the case of
\sigma=0
. A well-known work by Ebin
[
17
]
demonstrated the ill-posedness in the absence of surface tension. During the past two decades, water waves have been studied excessively, both in global and local solutions, for cases
\sigma=0
and
\sigma>0
. See
[
2
,
6
,
7
,
13
,
14
,
19
,
20
,
41
,
39
,
41
,
73
,
77
,
78
]
, etc. We refer readers to
[
40
]
for a survey on recent developments of the water waves.

On the other hand, for rotational initial data, Christodoulou and Lindblad
[
9
]
established the a priori estimate in the case when
\sigma=0
under the Taylor sign condition, followed by the well-posedness result by Lindblad
[
59
]
using the Nash-Moser iteration. Later, Coutand and Shkoller
[
10
]
proved the local well-posedness in both
\sigma>0
and
\sigma=0
cases. The readers may refer to Zhang and Zhang
[
83
]
, Shatah and Zeng
[
65
,
64
,
66
]
, Wang–Zhang–Zhao–Zheng
[
71
]
, and Ifrim–Pineau–Tataru–Taylor
[
37
]
for related topics. Additionally, we refer to
[
4
,
5
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
74
]
for the free-boundary problem in viscous fluids whose motion is described by the Navier–Stokes equations.

In recent years, significant progress has been made in the study of free boundary problems coupled with other equations. It is worth noting the free boundary Magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) problem. Hao and Luo
[
27
,
28
]
initially established a priori estimates and linearized local well-posedness for ideal incompressible MHD. Gu and Wang
[
23
]
demonstrated local well-posedness for the case of vanishing surface tension. Luo and Zhang
[
62
]
proved minimal regularity estimates of
H^{2.5+}
for a small fluid domain. Gu, Luo, and Zhang
[
22
]
were the first to establish local well-posedness for the non-zero surface tension case. Additionally, references are made to the works of Liu and Xin
[
61
]
and Ifrim–Pineau–Tataru–Taylor
[
36
]
for related topics. For compressible MHD, we recommend the works by Trakhinin and Wang
[
69
,
68
]
for ideal MHD with and without surface tension, and Zhang
[
81
,
82
]
for resistive MHD and its corresponding inviscid limit.

Most studies on nematic liquid crystals primarily focus on cases involving viscosity and fixed boundary conditions, while research on inviscid liquid crystals is relatively limited. Additionally, the local well-posedness of free boundary problems in complex fluids is quite complex, especially when surface tension is present, which complicates the process of closing the energy estimates. In this article, we establish the local well-posedness and derive energy estimates for the free boundary problem of inviscid liquid crystals without any loss of regularity. This work serves as a crucial step toward further exploration of general nematic liquid crystals influenced by viscosity, both experimentally and theoretically.

1.2
The Lagrangian description

We reformulate the equations into Lagrangian coordinates to study a fixed domain question. Let
\Omega:=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(0,1)
be a fixed domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})\in\Omega
be Lagrangian variables. Let
\eta(y,t):\Omega\to\mathcal{D}_{t}
be the solution of

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta(y,t)=u(\eta(y,t),t)

with initial condition

\displaystyle\eta(y,0)=Id.

We further set

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} v(y,t)&:=u(\eta(y,t),t),\\
q(y,t)&:=p(\eta(y,t),y),\\
\phi(y,t)&:=d(\eta(y,t),t)\\
A(y,t)&:=[D\eta]^{-1}\end{aligned}

to denote the material velocity, pressure, orientation vector field of nematic liquid crystal and inverse of the deformation tensor in Lagrangian coordinates respectively. Since the fluid is incompressible, the Jacobian
J=\det[D\eta]=1
in
\Omega\times[0,T]
. We introduce the moving top boundary and fixed bottom boundary respectively by
\Gamma=\{y_{3}=1\}
and
\Gamma_{0}=\{y_{3}=0\}
, and set
N
to be the normal outward direction of
\partial\Omega=\Gamma\cup\Gamma_{0}
.

On the fixed domain
\Omega
, the equations (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) can be reformulated as

\begin{cases}\eta=e+\int_{0}^{t}v\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\partial_{t}v_{i}+A_{i}^{j}\partial_{j}q=-A_{s}^{l}\partial_{l}(A_{s}^{m}\partial_{m}\phi_{n}A_{i}^{k}\partial_{k}\phi_{n})\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
A_{i}^{j}\partial_{j}v_{i}=0\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\partial_{t}\phi_{i}-A_{j}^{k}\partial_{k}(A_{j}^{m}\partial_{m}\phi_{i})=\phi_{i}(A_{s}^{l}\partial_{l}\phi_{n}A_{s}^{m}\partial_{m}\phi_{n})\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
v^{3}=0\quad\textit{on $\Gamma_{0}\times[0,T]$},\\
q\frac{A_{i}^{k}N_{k}}{|A_{j}^{l}N_{l}|}=-\sigma\Delta_{h}(\eta)\quad\text{on $\Gamma\times[0,T]$},\\
A_{i}^{l}N_{l}A_{i}^{k}\partial_{k}\phi=0\quad\text{on $\partial\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
(\eta,v,\phi)=(e,u_{0},d_{0})\quad\text{on $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$},\\
\end{cases}

(1.6)

where
e
is the identity map,
N^{T}=(0,0,1)
denotes the unit normal to
\Gamma
,
h_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}\eta\cdot\partial_{\beta}\eta
represents the induced metric on
\Gamma
,
\Delta_{h}
serves as the corresponding boundary Laplacian operator and the Einstein summation convention is used.
v_{i}=v^{i}
and
\phi_{i}=\phi^{i}
are understood as the i-th competent of
u
and
d
in Euler setting. To clear the symbol, we a little abuse the notation by introducing the differential operator
\nabla_{i}=A_{i}^{j}\partial_{j}
,
\nabla=(\nabla_{1},\nabla_{2},\nabla_{3})
and
\Delta_{g}=\delta^{jl}A_{j}^{k}\partial_{k}(A_{l}^{m}\partial_{m})
. Since the orientation vector fields which we study in this paper only occur in pairs, the index of corresponding fields will be suppressed to simplify the expression if it will not cause any confusion. (i.e. Use
\nabla_{k}\phi^{i}\nabla_{l}\phi^{j}\delta_{ij}=\nabla_{k}\phi\nabla_{l}\phi
to save notation.) Owing to the property of the incompressible flow,
J=1
. We obtain
\Delta_{g}=\partial_{i}(g^{ij}\partial_{j})
. Simultaneously, we use the symbol div and curl in following sense:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{div }v=\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}v_{j},\\
\text{$\text{div}_{A}$ }v=\nabla_{A}\cdot v=\delta^{ik}A_{i}^{j}\partial_{j}v_{k},\\
(\text{curl }v)^{i}=\varepsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}v_{k},\\
(\text{$\text{curl}_{A}$ }v)^{i}=\nabla_{A}\times v=\varepsilon^{ijk}A_{j}^{l}\partial_{l}v_{k}\end{aligned}

Under the above setting, we rewrite the equations (
1.6
) into:

\begin{cases}\eta=e+\int_{0}^{t}v\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\partial_{t}v+\nabla q=-\nabla\cdot(\nabla\phi\odot\nabla\phi)\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\nabla_{A}\cdot v=0\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\partial_{t}\phi-\Delta_{g}\phi=\phi|\nabla\phi|^{2}\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
v^{3}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma_{0}\times[0,T]$},\\
q\frac{A^{T}N}{|A^{T}N|}=-\sigma\Delta_{h}(\eta)\quad\text{on $\Gamma\times[0,T]$},\\
\nabla_{N}\phi=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma\times[0,T]$},\\
(\eta,v,\phi)=(e,u_{0},d_{0})\quad\text{on $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$},\\
\end{cases}

(1.7)

where
h
is the boundary metric induced by the metric
g
. Since
N=(0,0,1)
on the moving boundary, we can simply write
A_{j}^{3}A_{j}^{i}\partial_{i}\phi=g^{3i}\partial_{i}\phi=0
on
\Gamma
.

1.3
Main theorem

Theorem 1.1

(Main theorem)
.

Let
\sigma>0
be fixed. Assuming that
u_{0}\in H^{5.5}(\Omega)
with
\text{div }u_{0}=0
, and

\partial_{t}^{k}d(0)\in H^{5.5-k}(\Omega),\quad k=0,1,2,3,\quad\partial_{t}^{4}d(0)\in H^{1}(\Omega),

with
\nabla_{N}d_{0}=0
. Then there exists
T>0
, such that (
1.7
) admits a unique solution
(v,q,\phi)
, satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sup_{t\in[0,T]}&(\|\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}\|_{H^{6.5}}+\|v(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{5.5}(\Omega)}+\|\phi(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{5.5}(\Omega)}+\|q(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{4.5}(\Omega)}\\
&+\|v_{tttt}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)})+\|\partial_{t}^{5}\phi\|_{L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq M_{0},\end{aligned}

(1.8)

where
M_{0}
is a constant depending only on the
initial data.

Remark
.

The framework established by Coutand and Shkoller in
[
10
]
focuses on proving the local well-posedness of the free boundary problem coupled with the Euler equation. The existence result, dependent on initial data, is not optimal. Therefore, our objective does not aim for an optimal outcome with respect to the initial data
u_{0}
and
d_{0}
in this paper. For more refined results in the Lagrangian setting, readers may consult
[
3
]
and
[
15
]
.

1.4
Main difficulties and novelty of this article

The
L^{2}
-energy identity and a key cancellation scheme.

We begin with the following energy identity, which is derived from testing the second equation of (
1.7
) by
v
and then integrating in
\Omega
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|v|^{2}+\sigma\int_{\Gamma}\sqrt{h}h^{ij}\partial_{j}\eta^{k}\partial_{i}v^{k}&=\underbrace{\int_{\Omega}p\nabla\cdot v}_{\text{div }v=0}+\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla_{j}\phi\nabla_{i}v^{j}.\end{aligned}

(1.9)

Note that we can utilize the symmetry on the boundary:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sigma\int_{\Gamma}\frac{d}{dt}(\sqrt{h}h^{ij})\partial_{i}\eta^{k}\partial_{j}\eta^{k}&=\sigma\int_{\Gamma}\sqrt{h}(\frac{1}{2}h^{ij}h^{kl}-h^{lj}h^{ik})\partial_{t}(h_{kl})h_{ij}\\
&=\sigma\int_{\Gamma}\sqrt{h}h^{kl}\partial_{t}(h_{kl})-\sqrt{h}h^{lk}\partial_{t}(h_{kl})\\
&=0.\end{aligned}

(1.10)

Here,
h_{ij}=\partial_{i}\eta^{k}\partial_{j}\eta^{k}
is the induced boundary metric. We can rewrite the basis energy identity (
1.9
) as:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|v|^{2}+\sigma\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Gamma}\underbrace{h^{ij}\sqrt{h}\partial_{j}\eta^{k}\partial_{i}\eta^{k}}_{2\sqrt{h}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla_{j}\phi\nabla_{i}v^{j}.

(1.11)

The calculation involves some geometric identities, see Section 2 for details. The second term appearing on the RHS of (
1.11
) needs to be cancelled. By testing
\partial_{t}\phi
on the fourth equation of (
1.7
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}+\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{i}v^{k}\nabla_{k}\phi\nabla_{i}\phi+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}\phi\partial_{t}\phi|\nabla\phi|^{2}.

(1.12)

Owing to the identity
|\phi|=1
, we have
\phi\partial_{t}\phi=0
. Thus, we obtain the
L^{2}
-energy identity of
\phi
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{i}v^{k}\nabla_{k}\phi\nabla_{i}\phi.

(1.13)

Further, there would be a cancellation on the RHS of (
1.11
) and (
1.13
), which gives us

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|v|^{2}+\sigma\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Gamma}\sqrt{h}+\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{t}\phi|^{2}=0.

(1.14)

Additionally, in the higher-order energy, we will derive a similar energy estimate using cancellation. At the same time, we will treat the commutator terms as lower-order contributions. We denote
\partial_{t}
,
\partial_{1}
and
\partial_{2}
by
\bar{\partial}_{A}
. Taking
\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}
on the second equation (
1.7
), the higher order energy estimate reads

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega}|\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v|^{2}+\sigma\int_{\Gamma}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}(\sqrt{h}h^{ij}\partial_{j}\eta^{k})\partial_{i}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v^{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}p\nabla\cdot\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v+\int_{\Omega}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}\nabla_{i}(\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla_{j}\phi)\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v^{j}+\cdots,

(1.15)

where we used
\cdots
to represent the lower-order terms. The terms
\sigma\int_{\Gamma}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}(\sqrt{h}h^{ij}\partial_{j}\eta^{k})\partial_{i}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v^{k}
and
\int_{\Omega}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}p\nabla\cdot\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v
have been estimated in
[
10
]
via a subtle cancellation scheme. See Section 6. In this article, we derive a new cancellation relationship for the last term of (
1.15
). The leading order term can be written as:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}\nabla_{i}(\nabla_{i}\phi\nabla_{j}\phi)\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v^{j}=-\int_{\Omega}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}(\nabla_{i}\phi)\nabla_{j}\phi\nabla_{i}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v^{j}-\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{i}\phi\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}(\nabla_{j}\phi)\nabla_{i}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v^{j}.

(1.16)

The first term on the RHS of (
1.16
) can be controlled directly through the parabolic estimate of
\Delta_{A}\phi
. The cancellation scheme for the second term,
\int_{\Omega}\nabla_{i}\phi\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}(\nabla_{j}\phi)\nabla_{i}\bar{\partial}_{A}^{4}v^{j}
, is a novel contribution of this article and serves as the key lemma in Section 4.

No additional regularity for
\phi
due to the moving boundary.

The cancellation scheme is crucial because the regularity of the domain affects the parabolic estimate of
\phi
. While the regularizing effect of surface tension contributes additional derivatives in the
L^{2}
energy estimate, it is still insufficient to fully close the energy estimate. This results in a loss of
\frac{1}{2}
derivatives. Specifically, in the Lagrangian framework, the term involving
\Delta_{A}\phi
hinders the application of a standard parabolic estimate. As a result, the leading order of
\phi
is no longer two derivatives higher than
\partial_{t}\phi
and
v
. Instead, the leading-order energy of
\phi
is limited by the regularity of
\eta
.

Also, it is important to note that the regularizing effect does not yield additional derivatives. In other words, the regularity of
\eta
ultimately aligns with that of
v
. For examples of this, refer to
[
65
,
64
]
. In our case, this will be established through an a posteriori estimate. However, the regularizing effect is understood in the context of
L^{2}
estimates.

The approximate system.

We demonstrate the existence and uniqueness of solutions to the approximate system, along with the associated regularity. While our approach shares similarities with that of
[
10
]
, there are two key differences. First, we are required to construct a Picard iteration to establish the existence of a solution for the harmonic heat flow. Second, by mollifying the terms involving
\phi
and initial data
u_{0}
, we introduce an approximate system that extends the framework in
[
10
]
, with the added feature of an
L_{t}^{\infty}H^{12.5}
estimate for
v
.

1.5
Organization of this article

We devote Section 3 to constructing the approximate system and establishing the existence and uniqueness of its solutions.
In Section 4, we establish a uniform estimate for
\phi
with respect to
\kappa
. A key cancellation is derived in this section, which will be utilized in Section 6. In Sections 5 and 6, most of the calculations follow the framework of
[
10
]
. However, there are two notable differences. First, we provide a more precise estimate for
\|\partial_{t}^{3}p\|_{1}
. Second, the key cancellation derived in Section 4 is applied to the
\partial_{t}^{4}
-estimate in Section 6. Since the
\partial_{t}^{4}
-estimate in
[
10
]
involves subtle dependencies on other energy norms, we provide detailed proofs for the reader’s convenience and verification.

The following list of notations will be frequently used throughout this manuscript.

List of notations

1.

\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(0,1)
.
\Gamma=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{x_{3}=1\}
is the free boundary and
\Gamma_{0}=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{x_{3}=0\}
.

2.

The Sobolev norms in
\Omega
:
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}=\|\cdot\|_{s}
. For
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(0,T;H^{s}(\Omega))}=\|\cdot\|_{L_{t}^{p}H^{s}(\Omega)}
.

3.

The Sobolev norms on
\partial\Omega
:
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}=|\cdot|_{s}
. For
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(0,T;H^{s}(\Omega))}=\|\cdot\|_{L_{t}^{p}H^{s}(\Gamma)}
.

4.

\mathcal{P}(\cdot)
: a generic non-decreasing continuous function.

5.

\bar{\partial}=\partial_{1}
or
\partial_{2}
.
\bar{\Delta}=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
.

2
Preliminary results

2.1
Sobolev space and the dual space

In this paper, we will frequently use fractional Sobolev space. Since our domain is smooth, most common definitions on fractional Sobolev space coincide here. The reader may refer to
[
1
]
,
[
46
]
,
[
60
]
,
[
67
]
and the references therein. When
s>0
, the space
H^{-s}
denotes the dual space of
H^{s}
. We will use
(H_{0}^{1})^{*}
to represent the dual space of
H_{0}^{1}
. We need the Kato-Ponce inequality (the fractional Leibnitz rule) to derive some inequalities later.

Lemma 2.1

(Kato-Ponce inequality)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth bounded domain. For
s\geq 0
,
2\leq p_{1},q_{2}<\infty
satisfying the relationship
\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{2}}
,
f\in W^{s,p_{1}}(\Omega)\cap L^{q_{1}}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{p_{2}}(\Omega)\cap W^{s,q_{2}}(\Omega)
,
fg\in H^{s}(\Omega)
with the following estimate

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s,p_{1}}(\Omega)}\|g\|_{L^{p_{2}}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\Omega)}\|g\|_{W^{s,q_{2}}(\Omega)}.

(2.1)

In particular, owing to the Sobolev inequality, we have the following estimates

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{H^{0.5}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{0.5}(\Omega)}\|g\|_{H^{1.5+}(\Omega)},

(2.2)

and

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{H^{0.5}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\|g\|_{H^{1.5}(\Omega)}.

(2.3)

Proof
.

See
[
21
]
,
[
42
]
and
[
48
]
with appropriate modifications. (Sobolev extension with Kato-Ponce or Kato-Ponce with constrain) Actually, we only need the last two inequalities in our estimate, a rough version. Thus, one may also prove it via interpolation.

We denote the horizontal derivative by
\bar{\partial}=\partial_{1}
or
\partial_{2}
. In the context of the Lions-Magenes space, it is important to note that
\partial
is not, in general, a continuous operator from
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega)
to
H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega)
. However, in our estimate, we will only encounter
\bar{\partial}
in cases where “integrating 0.5 derivative by part” is required. To be precise, we need the following lemma

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
F\in H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\|\bar{\partial}F\|_{H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega)}\leq C\|F\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega)},

(2.4)

for some generic constant
C
.

Proof
.

See
[
10
]
and
[
11
]
.

Remark
.

Instead of relying on this lemma, we could alternatively define
\bar{\partial}=(1-\bar{\Delta})^{\frac{1}{2}}
, which provides a more convenient framework for handling a 0.5 derivative. However, this approach would require several additional and complex commutator estimates for the boundary analysis. For the sake of simplicity and convenience, we will continue to define
\bar{\partial}=\partial_{1}
or
\partial_{2}
.

2.2
Horizontal convolution by layers

While discussing functions on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, we always view them as periodic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with norms evaluated over a single period. Except for
\eta
, the functions
v
,
q
, and
\phi
are periodic in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Although
\eta
itself is not periodic, its derivative,
\partial\eta
, is periodic. Throughout this paper, the norm of
\eta
will always be taken over one period.

Definition 2.1

(Horizontal mollifier)
.

For a fixed
\kappa_{0}>0
which is sufficiently small, we choose
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
. Let
f
be an
L^{p}
function (
1\leq p\leq\infty
) defined on
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(0,1)
, or a function such as
\eta\in L_{\text{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\kappa}f(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho_{\kappa}(x_{1}-y_{1},x_{2}-y_{2})f(y_{1},y_{2},x_{3})dy_{1}dy_{2},

(2.5)

where
\rho_{\kappa}=c\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\rho(\frac{y_{1}}{\kappa},\frac{y_{2}}{\kappa})
is a standard two dimensional smooth Friedrich mollifier (radial symmetric and
\|\rho_{\kappa}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=1
).

The following properties about the mollifier are well-known.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
f
is a periodic function such that the norms in the following inequalities are well-defined.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\Lambda_{\kappa}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim_{\kappa}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\kappa\|f\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\kappa\|f\|_{H^{1.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|f\|_{H^{0.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{H^{0.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|f\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|f\|_{H^{1.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\kappa\|f\|_{H^{2.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\end{aligned}

(2.6)

Proof
.

See
[
10
]
and
[
22
]
. We provide a sketchy proof for the last five inequalities.
Using the definition, (taking
\tilde{f}
as
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\Lambda_{\kappa}\tilde{f}-\tilde{f})(x)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{\kappa}(y)(\tilde{f}(x-y)-\tilde{f}(x))dy\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(y)(\tilde{f}(x-\kappa y)-\tilde{f}(x))dy\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(y)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{f}(x-t\kappa y)dtdy\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-\kappa y\rho(y)\int_{0}^{1}D\tilde{f}(x-t\kappa y)dtdy\end{aligned}

(2.7)

Invoking Minkowski inequality and invariance of translation of
L^{p}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|(\Lambda_{\kappa}\tilde{f}-\tilde{f})(x)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\kappa y\rho(y)\|D\tilde{f}(x-t\kappa y)\|_{L_{x}^{2}}dydt\\
&\lesssim\kappa\|D\tilde{f}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})},\end{aligned}

(2.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|(\Lambda_{\kappa}\tilde{f}-\tilde{f})(x)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\kappa y\rho(y)\|D\tilde{f}(x-t\kappa y)\|_{L_{x}^{4}}dydt\\
&\lesssim\kappa\|D\tilde{f}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})},\end{aligned}

(2.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|(\Lambda_{\kappa}\tilde{f}-\tilde{f})(x)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}&\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\kappa y\rho(y)\|D\tilde{f}(x-t\kappa y)\|_{L_{x}^{\infty}}dydt\\
&\lesssim\kappa\|D\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}.\end{aligned}

(2.10)

Therefore, the Sobolev embedding theorem implies that

\displaystyle\|(\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f)(x)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lesssim\kappa\|f\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\|(\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f)(x)\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lesssim\kappa\|f\|_{H^{1.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})},

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|(\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f)(x)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lesssim\kappa\|f\|_{H^{2.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

(2.13)

Similarly, invoking the Morrey’s estimate, (in our case
n=2
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |(\Lambda_{\kappa}\tilde{f}-\tilde{f})(x)|&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{\kappa}(y)|\tilde{f}(x-y)-\tilde{f}(x)|dy\\
&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{\kappa}(y)\|D\tilde{f}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\|\frac{1}{r^{n-1}}\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}}(B_{\kappa}(0))}dy\\
&\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{\kappa}(y)\sqrt{\kappa}\|\tilde{f}\|_{H^{1.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}dy\\
&\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|\tilde{f}\|_{H^{1.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})},\end{aligned}

(2.14)

Thus,

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|f\|_{H^{1.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

(2.15)

The inequality involving
H^{0.5}
is an application of interpolation.
We verify this property via an equivalent definition of fractional Sobolev space (See
[
1
]
,
[
46
]
,
[
60
]
,
[
67
]
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\Lambda_{\kappa}\tilde{f}-\tilde{f})(x)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{\kappa}(x-y)\frac{(\tilde{f}(y)-\tilde{f}(x))}{|y-x|^{\frac{3}{2}}}|y-x|^{\frac{3}{2}}dy\\
&\lesssim(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\rho_{\kappa}(x-y)|y-x|^{\frac{3}{2}}|^{2}dy)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}|\frac{(\tilde{f}(y)-\tilde{f}(x))}{|y-x|^{\frac{3}{2}}}|^{2}dy)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&=(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\kappa\rho(x-y)|x-y|^{3}dy)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\frac{|\tilde{f}(y)-\tilde{f}(x)|^{2}}{|y-x|^{3}}dy)^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{aligned}

(2.16)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\Lambda_{\kappa}\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|\tilde{f}(y)-\tilde{f}(x)|^{2}}{|y-x|^{3}}dydx)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|\tilde{f}\|_{H^{0.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}.\end{aligned}

(2.17)

Using a similar method, we can verify that

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|f\|_{H^{1.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

(2.18)

Hence, we can infer
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f-f\|_{H^{0.5}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|f\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}
from interpolation.

Remark
.

Since we always calculate the norm in one period, a similar argument is also valid for
\eta
.

Lemma 2.4

(Commutation type lemma)
.

Let
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for
s>1
and
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Then there exists a constant C independent on
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
s.t.

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\kappa}(fg)-f\Lambda_{\kappa}g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq C\kappa\|f\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

(2.19)

In particular, suppose
f\in H^{s}(\Omega)
for
s>1
and
g\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, the trace inequality implies that

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\kappa}(fg)-f\Lambda_{\kappa}g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\kappa\|f\|_{H^{s+1.5}(\Omega)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(2.20)

Further suppose
f\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\kappa}(f\bar{\partial}g)-f\Lambda_{\kappa}\bar{\partial}g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

(2.21)

Proof
.

See
[
10
]
and
[
11
]
.

2.3
Elliptic estimates and trace estimates

Lemma 2.5

(Quasi-linear elliptic estimate)
.

Let
s
be an real number such that
1\leq s\leq 5.5
. Define an elliptic operator
L_{A}
and corresponding Neumann boundary operator as

\displaystyle L_{A}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}(g^{ij}\partial_{j}),

(2.22)

\displaystyle B_{A}

\displaystyle=A_{i}^{j}A_{i}^{k}N_{j}\partial_{k}

(2.23)

Let
f\in H^{s-2}(\Omega)
and
g\in H^{s-1.5}(\Gamma)
. Suppose
u\in H^{s}(\Omega)
is the solution of the following quasi-linear elliptic problem with Neumann condition.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L_{A}u&=f\quad\textit{in $\Omega$},\\
B_{A}u&=g\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega$}.\end{aligned}

(2.24)

Then tho following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{s}\mathcal{P}(\|\eta\|_{H^{5.5}(\Omega)})\|f\|_{H^{s-2}(\Omega)}+\|g\|_{H^{s-\frac{3}{2}}(\partial\Omega)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(2.25)

Proof
.

A proof for the integer case can be found in
[
16
]
. The readers may derive the desired estimate through interpolation. Since our focus is on obtaining a solution over a short time interval, an alternative approach in our case is to consider:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta u&=\partial_{i}((\delta^{ij}-g^{ij})\partial_{j}u)+f\quad\textit{in $\Omega$},\\
\partial_{3}u&=(\delta^{3i}-g^{3i})\partial_{i}u+g\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega$}.\end{aligned}

(2.26)

See
[
15
]
and the discussion therein.

We will utilize the following well-known curl-div decomposition.

Lemma 2.6

(Hodge elliptic decomposition)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an
H^{r+1}
domain with boundary
\Gamma
,
r>1.5
. If
F\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
\text{curl }F\in H^{s-1}(\Omega)
,
\text{div}\,F\in H^{s-1}(\Omega)
, and
\bar{\partial}F\cdot N|_{\Gamma}\in H^{s-\frac{3}{2}}(\Gamma)
for
1\leq s\leq r
, then there exists a constant C depending only on
\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\|F\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}\leq C(\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega}+\|\text{curl }F\|_{H^{s-1}(\Omega)}+\|\text{div}\,F\|_{H^{s-1}(\Omega)}+\|\bar{\partial}F\cdot N\|_{H^{s-\frac{3}{2}}(\Gamma)}).

(2.27)

Proof
.

See
[
8
]
.

2.4
Geometric Identity

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
n
be the unit outer normal to
\eta(\Gamma)
. We denote
\Pi=n\otimes n
to be the normal projection operator. Let
\bar{\partial}_{A}
represent the
\bar{\partial}
or
\partial_{t}
. We have the following geometric identities:

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=\frac{A_{.}^{3}}{|A_{.}^{3}|},

(2.28)

\displaystyle\partial A_{i}^{j}

\displaystyle=-A_{i}^{l}\partial_{l}\partial\eta^{k}A_{k}^{j},

(2.29)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}A_{i}^{j}

\displaystyle=-A_{i}^{l}\partial_{l}v^{k}A_{k}^{j},

(2.30)

\displaystyle\sqrt{h}

\displaystyle=|JA_{.}^{3}|,

(2.31)

\displaystyle\Pi_{j}^{i}

\displaystyle=\delta^{i}_{j}-h^{\alpha\beta}\bar{\partial}_{\alpha}\eta^{i}\bar{\partial}_{\beta}\eta^{l}\delta_{jl},

(2.32)

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}^{j}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{k}^{j}\Pi_{i}^{k},

(2.33)

\displaystyle\sqrt{h}\Delta_{h}(\eta^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h}h^{ij}\Pi_{\beta}^{\alpha}\bar{\partial}_{i}\bar{\partial}_{j}\eta^{\beta},

(2.34)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{A}(\sqrt{h}\Delta_{h}\eta^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}(\sqrt{h}h^{ij}(\delta^{\alpha\lambda}-h^{lk}\bar{\partial}_{l}\eta^{\lambda}\bar{\partial}_{k}\eta^{\alpha})\bar{\partial}_{j}\bar{\partial}_{A}\eta^{\lambda}+\sqrt{h}(h^{ij}h^{kl}-h^{ik}h^{jl})\bar{\partial}_{k}\eta^{\lambda}\bar{\partial}_{A}\bar{\partial}_{l}\eta^{\lambda}\bar{\partial}_{j}\eta^{\alpha}),

(2.35)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{A}n_{j}

\displaystyle=-h^{kl}\bar{\partial}_{k}\bar{\partial}_{A}\eta^{i}n_{i}\bar{\partial}_{l}\eta_{j}.

(2.36)

Proof
.

The proof of these formulas can be found in
[
10
]
and
[
15
]
.

3
The smoothed
\kappa
-approximate system and functional framework

Given
v\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\Omega))
, we introduce the approximating function
v^{\kappa}
by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta v^{\kappa}&=\Delta(\rho_{\kappa}*Ev)\quad\textit{in $\Omega$},\\
v^{\kappa}&=\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega$},\end{aligned}

(3.1)

where
\rho_{\kappa}
is standard mollifier on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Then, we define

\displaystyle\eta^{\kappa}=e+\int_{0}^{T}v^{\kappa}

(3.2)

Lemma 3.1
.

For some nonnegative integer
k
and positive integer
l
such that the following norms are well-defined, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\partial^{l}\partial_{t}^{k}v^{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{l}(\Omega)},\\
\|\partial^{l}\partial_{t}^{k}v^{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&\lesssim_{\kappa}\|\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)},\\
\|\eta^{\kappa}\|_{H^{l}(\Omega)}&\lesssim\|\eta\|_{H^{l}(\Omega)}+\|e_{0}^{\kappa}\|_{H^{l}(\Omega)},\\
\|\partial_{t}^{k}(v^{\kappa}-v)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}&\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|v\|_{H^{1.5}(\Omega)}.\end{aligned}

(3.3)

Proof
.

Note that if we only require
E
to be a strong
m
-extension, it can be defined using the reflection method. In this case,
\partial_{t}
can be extended outside
\Omega
and commutes with the extension operator. Thus, the first two inequalities is a direct consequence of elliptic estimate. Integrating on time, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta\eta^{\kappa}&=\Delta(\rho_{\kappa}*E\eta)-\Delta e^{\kappa}\quad\textit{in $\Omega$},\\
\eta^{\kappa}&=\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega$}.\end{aligned}

(3.4)

Therefore, the elliptic estimate gives us the third estimate.
For the fourth estimate, we can compute that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|(\rho_{\kappa}*E\partial_{t}^{k}v)-\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}&\lesssim\|\rho_{\kappa}*E\partial_{t}^{k}v-E\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|E\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{1.5}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{1.5}(\Omega)},\\
\|\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}v-\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega)}&\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{1}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{1.5}(\Omega)}.\end{aligned}

(3.5)

It immediately yields that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{k}(v^{\kappa}-v)\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}\|v\|_{H^{1.5}(\Omega)}.

(3.6)

An important property is that
\eta^{\kappa}=\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta
on
\partial\Omega
, which preserves the doubly horizontally mollified structure on the boundary.

Definition 3.1
.

For a fixed small
\kappa_{0}
, for any
\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0})
, we define the following notations:

\displaystyle A^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=[D\eta^{\kappa}]^{-1},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\nabla^{\kappa}_{i}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A^{\kappa}})_{i}=(A^{\kappa})_{i}^{j}\partial_{j}

(3.8)

\displaystyle\nabla^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{A^{\kappa}}=(\nabla^{\kappa}_{1},\nabla^{\kappa}_{2},\nabla^{\kappa}_{3})

(3.9)

\displaystyle g_{\kappa}^{ij}

\displaystyle=(A^{\kappa})_{l}^{i}(A^{\kappa})_{l}^{j}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}(g_{\kappa}^{ij}\partial_{j})=\partial_{i}((A^{\kappa})_{l}^{i}(A^{\kappa})_{l}^{j}\partial_{j})

(3.11)

\displaystyle\nabla_{N}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{A^{\kappa}})_{N}=\delta^{ik}(A^{\kappa})_{i}^{j}N_{j}(A^{\kappa})_{k}^{l}\partial_{l}

(3.12)

\displaystyle J_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\det\nabla\eta^{\kappa}.

(3.13)

Now,
we introduce the following
\kappa
-approximate system of (
1.7
):

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\eta=e+\int_{0}^{t}v\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\partial_{t}v+\nabla^{\kappa}q=-\partial_{t}\phi^{\kappa}\cdot\nabla^{\kappa}\phi^{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\kappa}|\nabla^{\kappa}\phi^{\kappa}|^{2}\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\nabla^{\kappa}\cdot v=0\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\partial_{t}\phi-\Delta_{g}^{\kappa}\phi=\phi|\nabla^{\kappa}\phi|^{2}\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
v^{3}=0\quad\text{on $\Gamma_{0}\times[0,T]$},\\
q=-\sigma(\frac{\sqrt{h}}{\sqrt{h_{\kappa}}}\Delta_{h}(\eta)\cdot n_{\kappa})+\kappa\frac{1}{\sqrt{h_{\kappa}}}((1-\bar{\Delta})(v\cdot n^{\kappa}))\quad\text{on $\Gamma\times[0,T]$},\\
\nabla_{N}^{\kappa}\phi=0\quad\text{on $\partial\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
(\eta,v,\phi)=(e^{\kappa},u_{0},d_{0})\quad\text{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$}.\end{cases}

(3.14)

Later, we denote
(1-\bar{\Delta})
by
\bar{\Delta}_{0}
.

\phi^{\kappa}
is the mollified
\phi
by
\rho_{\kappa}*E\phi
constrained on
\Omega
, where
E
is the Sobolev extension operator.

Here, despite releasing the constrains on
\phi
(i.e. we don’t need to keep
|\phi^{\kappa}|=1
) in first velocity equation, we remain the form of
\partial_{t}\phi^{\kappa}\cdot\nabla^{\kappa}\phi^{\kappa}
. i.e.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+\nabla^{\kappa}q=-\partial_{t}\phi^{\kappa}\cdot\nabla^{\kappa}\phi^{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\kappa}|\nabla^{\kappa}\phi^{\kappa}|^{2}

(3.15)

Remark
.

Later, we will utilize a cancellation structure to derive a uniform-in-
\kappa
energy estimate. The term
\nabla^{\kappa}|\nabla^{\kappa}\phi^{\kappa}|^{2}
will contribute a leading-order term. If we retain
\nabla^{\kappa}(\nabla^{\kappa}\phi^{\kappa}\odot\nabla^{\kappa}\phi^{\kappa})
, a similar proof for the cancellation can still be established. However, the curl estimate will become more lengthy and involve additional error analysis.

Recall that if we merely require
E
to be a strong
m
extension (m large enough), we can define
E\phi
via the reflection method. Consequently, following a similar reason,
\partial_{t}E\phi
is well-defined provided that the regularity of
\phi
is enough, and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}E\phi=E\partial_{t}\phi.

(3.16)

This ensures that
\partial_{t}\phi^{\kappa}
inherits the characteristics of standard mollification: for suitable positive integer
k,l
such that the norms all well-defined

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\partial^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}\phi^{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&\lesssim\|\partial^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}(\rho_{\kappa}*E\phi^{\kappa})\|_{L^{2}}=\|\rho_{\kappa}*\partial^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}E\phi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|E\partial_{t}^{l}\phi\|_{H^{k}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{l}\phi\|_{H^{k}(\Omega)}\\
\|\partial^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}\phi^{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&\lesssim\|\partial^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}(\rho_{\kappa}*E\phi^{\kappa})\|_{L^{2}}=\|\partial^{k}\rho_{\kappa}*E\partial_{t}^{l}\phi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{\kappa}\|E\partial_{t}^{l}\phi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{l}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
\|\partial_{t}^{l}\phi-\partial_{t}^{l}\phi^{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}&\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{l}E\phi-\partial_{t}^{l}\rho_{\kappa}*E\phi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\kappa\|E\partial_{t}^{l}\phi\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\kappa\|\partial_{t}^{l}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.\end{aligned}

(3.17)

Proposition 3.2
.

For each fixed
\kappa
, there exists a unique solution
(\eta,v,\phi)
to (
3.14
) with
T_{\kappa}>0
depending on
\|u_{0}\|_{H^{13.5}(\Omega)}
and
\|d_{0}\|_{H^{9}(\Omega)}
, provided that
\nabla_{N}d_{0}=0
on the boundary. Further, we have the following estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{5}\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{t}^{k}\phi\|_{H^{10-2k}(\Omega)}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{4}\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{H^{13.5-3k}(\Omega)}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{3}\|\partial_{t}^{k}p\|_{H^{14.5-3k}(\Omega)}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{4}\sup_{t}\|\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{12.5-3k}^{2}\lesssim C(\kappa,u_{0},d_{0}).

(3.18)

Remark
.

This
T_{\kappa}
should be interpreted as a short time duration independent of other parameters that will emerge in Section 3. The energy estimate in Section 4 will demonstrate that a small
T
can be attained, also independent of
\kappa
. This
T
can be regarded as the consistent time interval throughout our paper, enabling us to employ a compactness argument at every step of the proof.

Proof
.

For
T>0
, we introduce a closed convex subset of
L^{2}(0,T;H^{13.5}(\Omega))
as

\displaystyle C_{T}=\{v\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{13.5}(\Omega))|\|v\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{13.5}(\Omega)}\leq N_{0},v_{0}=u_{0}\}

We set the compatible conditions as follows:

Given
v_{0},d_{0}
, we require
\partial_{t}d(0)=\Delta d_{0}+d_{0}|\nabla d|^{2}
.
p(0)
will be obtained via the elliptic equation:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta p(0)&=-\nabla u_{0}:\nabla u_{0}-\nabla\cdot(\partial_{t}d^{\kappa}(0)\nabla d_{0}^{\kappa})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta|\nabla d_{0}^{\kappa}|^{2}\quad\textit{in $\Omega$},\\
p(0)&=\kappa(1-\bar{\Delta})(u_{0}^{3})\quad\textit{on $\Gamma_{1}$},\\
\nabla_{N}p(0)&=-\partial_{t}d^{\kappa}(0)\nabla^{\kappa}_{N}d^{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\kappa}_{N}|\nabla^{\kappa}d^{\kappa}|^{2}\quad\textit{on $\Gamma_{0}$}.\end{aligned}

(3.19)

\partial_{t}v(0)=-\nabla p(0)-\partial_{t}d_{0}^{\kappa}\nabla d_{0}^{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla|\nabla d_{0}^{\kappa}|^{2}
. Inductively, after establishing the existence of
\phi
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
v
,
\partial_{t}v
and
p
, we define the initial values for their respective higher time derivatives. This inductive procedure is always valid, we don’t need to worry about the existence of such a system.

For the sake of convenience, we always take
T\leq 1
. In the following section,
\bar{A}
,
\bar{A}^{\kappa}
corresponds to
\bar{v}
. We further choose sufficiently small time interval such that
\sup_{t}(\|(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{j}-\delta_{i}^{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\|(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{j}-\delta_{i}^{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)})\leq\varepsilon_{1}
, where
\varepsilon_{1}
is a sufficient small number.
N_{0}
will be determined later and will depend only on the initial data. If necessary, we may apply
T\leq 1
to determine such an
N_{0}
. (i.e.,
N_{0}
is independent of the time interval) Similarly,
T
will also be determined later and will depend solely on the initial data and
\kappa
in Section 3.
To make use of the estimate in
[
10
]
, we establish the following scheme:

1) Given
\bar{v}\in C_{T}
, we proved that there exists a unique solution
\phi
for the equation:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\phi-\Delta_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi=\phi|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi|^{2},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}\phi=0,\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\phi=d_{0},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$}\end{cases}

(3.20)

2)Provided with the same
\phi
, denote
|\phi|^{2}-1
by
G
. We prove that there exists a unique solution of

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}G-\Delta_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}G=G|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi|^{2},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}G=0,\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
G=0,\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$}.\end{cases}

(3.21)

As a direct consequence, we can show
|\phi|=1
.

3)Plunge
\phi
into the penalized problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v^{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}q^{\varepsilon}=-\partial_{t}\phi^{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi^{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi^{\kappa}|^{2},\quad\text{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\cdot v^{\varepsilon}=-\varepsilon q^{\varepsilon},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
(v^{\varepsilon})^{3}=0,\quad\text{on $\Gamma_{0}\times[0,T]$},\\
q^{\varepsilon}\bar{n}^{\kappa}_{i}=-\sigma(\frac{\sqrt{\bar{h}}}{\sqrt{\bar{h}_{\kappa}}}\Delta_{\bar{h}}(\bar{\eta})\cdot\bar{n}^{\kappa})\bar{n}^{\kappa}_{i}+\kappa\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bar{h}_{\kappa}}}(1-\bar{\Delta})(v^{\varepsilon}\cdot\bar{n}^{\kappa})\bar{n}^{\kappa}_{i},\quad\text{on $\Gamma\times[0,T]$},\\
(\eta^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon})=(Id,u_{0}),\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$}.\end{cases}

(3.22)

After using a standard Galerkin method to establish the existence of a weak solution for the penalized problem, we will perform a uniform
\varepsilon
-estimate similar to the case in
[
10
]
.

4)After a compactness argument, we will show the existence of a weak solution to the linearized
\kappa
-problem and the corresponding regularity.

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v+\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}q=-\partial_{t}\phi^{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi^{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi^{\kappa}|^{2},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\cdot v=0,\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$},\\
v^{3}=0,\quad\text{on $\Gamma_{0}\times[0,T]$},\\
q\bar{n}^{\kappa}_{i}=-\sigma(\frac{\sqrt{\bar{h}}}{\sqrt{\bar{h}_{\kappa}}}\Delta_{\bar{h}}(\bar{\eta})\cdot\bar{n})\bar{n}^{\kappa}_{i}+\kappa\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bar{h}_{\kappa}}}(1-\bar{\Delta})(v\cdot\bar{n}^{\kappa})\bar{n}_{i}^{\kappa},\quad\text{on $\Gamma\times[0,T]$},\\
(\eta,v)=(Id,u_{0}),\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$}.\end{cases}

(3.23)

5) Finally, using a Tychonoff-type fixed point theorem, we will prove the existence of a unique solution for the
\kappa
-approximating problem (
3.14
).

3.1
Proof of (
3.20
)

By a slight abuse of notation, in the following section, we will use
\phi
to denote the solutions to different equations. The meaning of those variables is clear in the context.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists a unique solution
\phi
to the following system:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\phi-\Delta_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi=F_{1},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}\phi=F_{2},\quad\textit{on $\Gamma\times[0,T]$,}\\
\phi=F_{0},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$},\end{cases}

(3.24)

provided with
F_{0}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
,
F_{1}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
, and
F_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega))
. Further, we have the estimate:

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\Omega))}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}\lesssim\|F_{1}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\|F_{2}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega))}+\|F_{0}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}.

(3.25)

Proof
.

This lemma is widely known in standard parabolic theory, based on the Galerkin method. For detailed steps, Chapter III of
[
43
]
can be consulted. (Actually, Theorem 5.1 with a slight modification).

Proposition 3.4
.

There exists a small constant
T_{1}
and a unique solution
\phi
of equation (
3.20
) within the time interval
t\in(0,T_{1})
. Furthermore, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{t}(\|\phi\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)})+\|\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{4}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}^{2}\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim M_{0},

(3.26)

where
M_{0}
is a constant that only depends on
\|d_{0}\|_{H^{3}}
and
N_{0}
.

Proof
.

Given the sufficiently high regularity of
\bar{v}
in this proposition, we will omit the constant associated with
\bar{v}
. For example, we will write directly

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi\|_{H^{k}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|\bar{A}^{\kappa}\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}\|\phi\|_{H^{k+1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{H^{k+1}(\Omega)},\quad\textit{k=0,1,2,3}.

(3.27)

Set

\displaystyle S_{1}=\{\phi|\|\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{4}_{x}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{x}^{2}(\Omega)}+\sup_{t}(\|\phi\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)})\leq M_{0},\phi|_{t=0}=\phi_{0},\partial_{t}\phi|_{t=0}=\Delta d_{0}+d_{0}|\nabla d_{0}|^{2}\}.

(3.28)

M_{0}
is to be determined later. Clearly,
S_{1}
is a Banach space.
Now, given
\tilde{\phi}\in S_{1}
, we solve the linear system:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\phi-\Delta_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi=\tilde{\phi}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}|^{2},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}\phi=0,\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\phi=d_{0},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$}\end{aligned}

(3.29)

Utilizing the previous lemma, we can show the existence of
\phi
as a direct consequence of the following estimate:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}|\tilde{\phi}|^{2}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}|^{4}&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{4}(\Omega)}^{4}\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{4}\\
&\lesssim T\mathcal{P}(M_{0}).\end{aligned}

(3.30)

Thus,

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(M_{0})+\|d_{0}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}

(3.31)

Taking
\partial_{t}
on (
3.29
), denoting
\partial_{t}\phi
by
\psi_{1}
, we can verify that
\psi_{1}
is a weak solution to the following system: (Later, we will see the uniqueness of the solution. Thus, we use the same symbol to represent the variable and
\partial_{t}\phi
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\psi_{1}-\Delta_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\psi_{1}&=\partial_{t}(\tilde{\phi}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}|^{2})+[\partial_{t},\Delta_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}]\phi,\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}\psi_{1}&=-[\partial_{t},\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}]\partial_{i}\phi,\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\psi_{1}&=\Delta d_{0}+d_{0}|\nabla d_{0}|^{2},\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$}\end{aligned}

(3.32)

And, by the Sobolev inequality, it indicates that:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\partial_{t}(\tilde{\phi}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}|^{2})\|_{L^{2}_{t}L_{x}^{2}}&\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\phi}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}|^{2}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L_{x}^{2}}+\|\tilde{\phi}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}\partial_{t}(\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi})\|_{L^{2}_{t}L_{x}^{2}}\\
&\lesssim(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{6}}^{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}+(\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\partial_{t}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(M_{0}).\end{aligned}

(3.33)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|[\partial_{t},\Delta_{\bar{A^{\kappa}}}]\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\Omega)}=\|\partial_{i}\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{ij}\partial_{j}\phi+\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(M_{0})+\|d_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}.\end{aligned}

(3.34)

For the boundary term
F_{2}=-\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}\phi
, the trace inequality implies that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{2}_{x}(\Omega)}\|\partial\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{1}_{x}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(M_{0})+\|d_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}.

(3.35)

Hence, combining Lemma 3.2, (
3.33
), (
3.34
), and (
3.35
), we have the existence and uniqueness of the solution and the following estimate:

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\|\psi_{1}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\psi_{1}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\psi_{1}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C_{T}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(M_{0})+\|d_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}).

(3.36)

Owing to our setting
T\leq 1
,
C_{T}
can be bounded by a constant
C
independent of time. Subsequently, we will consistently consider it as a constant without elaboration in analogous scenarios. We can directly check that
\partial_{t}\phi
is also the weak solution to (
3.32
). Now, we can calculate that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\phi}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}|\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{3}(\Omega)})\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(M_{0}),

(3.37)

the standard elliptic estimate gives us

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{4}(\Omega)}+\|\phi\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{3}(\Omega)}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(M_{0})+\|d_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}

(3.38)

Hence, combining Lemma 3.2, (
3.30
), (
3.36
), and (
3.38
) we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t}(\|\phi\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)})+\|\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{4}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}^{2}\phi\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{P}(M_{0})+\|d_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}.

(3.39)

Now, selecting sufficiently large
M_{0}
compared with
\|d_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}
, and choosing sufficiently small
T_{1}
, the solution of (
3.29
) defines a map
M:S_{1}\to S_{1}
. To establish the existence theorem, it suffices to show that
M
is a contraction. We still use
\phi^{(i)},\tilde{\phi}^{(i)}\in S_{1}
,
i=1,2
to express the relation
\phi^{(i)}=M(\tilde{\phi}^{(i)})
.
Let
\Psi=\phi^{(1)}-\phi^{(2)}
and
\tilde{\Psi}=\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}-\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}
; then we obtain:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\Psi-\Delta_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\Psi&=\tilde{\Psi}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}|^{2}+\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\Psi}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}+\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}),\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}\Psi&=0,\quad\textit{on $\partial\Omega\times[0,T]$,}\\
\Psi&=0,\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times\{t=0\}$}\end{aligned}

(3.40)

We will adopt a similar strategy to that of considering
\partial_{t}\Psi
. From a direct calculation, it yields that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\tilde{\Psi}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}|^{2}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H_{x}^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}(\tilde{\Psi}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}|^{2})\|_{L_{t}^{2}L_{x}^{2}}&\lesssim(\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\Psi}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}\|_{H^{2}}^{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}+(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\Psi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}\|_{H^{2}}^{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&+(\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\Psi}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\|\partial_{t}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}\|_{H^{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}C(M_{0})(\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\Psi}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}}+\|\tilde{\Psi}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{2}}),\end{aligned}

(3.41)

and a similar computation shows that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\Psi}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}+\tilde{\phi}^{(2)})\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}(\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\Psi}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}+\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}))\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
\lesssim&(\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\Psi}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}+\tilde{\phi}^{(2)})\|_{H^{2}}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}+(\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\Psi}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}+\tilde{\phi}^{(2)})\|_{H^{2}}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
+&(\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\|\partial_{t}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\Psi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}+\tilde{\phi}^{(2)})\|_{H^{2}}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}+(\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\phi}^{(2)}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\tilde{\Psi}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\|\partial_{t}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\tilde{\phi}^{(1)}+\tilde{\phi}^{(2)})\|_{L^{2}}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
\lesssim&T^{\frac{1}{2}}C(M_{0})(\|\tilde{\Psi}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{3}}+\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\Psi}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{1}}).\end{aligned}

(3.42)

The estimate of commutator reads:

\displaystyle\|[\partial_{t},\Delta_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}]\Psi\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}}\lesssim\|\partial_{i}\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{ij}\partial_{j}\Psi+\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\Psi\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{t}\|\Psi\|_{H^{2}}.

(3.43)

The boundary term can be controlled as

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\kappa}^{3i}\partial_{i}\Psi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{t}\|\Psi\|_{H^{2}(\Omega)}.

(3.44)

Hence, combining (
3.41
), (
3.42
), (
3.43
), and (
3.44
)
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sup_{t}(\|\Psi\|_{H^{3}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\Psi\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)})+\|\Psi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{4}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\Psi\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{2}(\Omega)}\\
\lesssim&T^{\frac{1}{2}}C(M_{0})(\sup_{t}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\Psi}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\tilde{\Psi}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{3}(\Omega)})+T^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{t}\|\Psi\|_{H^{2}}.\end{aligned}

(3.45)

Taking
T
sufficiently small, we can ensure that
M
is a contraction. A standard argument then yields the existence of a solution and the desired estimates (Choosing a constant slightly larger than
M_{0}
, which we still denote it by
M_{0}
). This completes the proof of the proposition.

3.2
Proof of (
3.21
)

Now, we verify that
|\phi|=1
. Test
G
in (
3.21
),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\Omega}G^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bar{g}^{ij}\partial_{i}G\partial_{j}G&=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}G^{2}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi|^{2}.\end{aligned}

(3.46)

Invoking the Hölder inequality, Young inequality and the Sobolev embedding theorem, it implies that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sup_{t}\|G(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|G\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}&\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\|G\|_{L^{3}(\Omega)}^{2}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\|G\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|G\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\|G\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|G\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\mathcal{P}(N_{0},M_{0})\\
&\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|G\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|G\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))}\mathcal{P}(N_{0},M_{0}).\end{aligned}

(3.47)

Choosing
T
small enough, we can see that

\displaystyle G=0\quad\textit{in $\Omega\times[0,T]$}.

(3.48)

Consequently, we have
|\phi|=1
.

Remark
.

Alternatively, readers can refer to Theorem 3.2 in Chapter 3 of
[
43
]
, where it is not difficult to determine the regularity of
\phi
that meets the theorem’s conditions.

3.3
Proof of (
3.22
)

Let
H
denote
-\partial_{t}\phi^{\kappa}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi^{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}|\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\phi^{\kappa}|^{2}
. The term related to
\phi^{\kappa}
can be estimated using the properties of convolution, and
H
can be regarded as smooth. The calculations in
[
10
]
are sufficient to justify the existence of the system. Here, we provide a sketchy proof, offering detailed explanations only where necessary modifications are required. Taking
(e_{l})_{l=1}^{\infty}
as a basis of
H^{1.5}(\Omega)
and an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
, we define the approximating function

\displaystyle w_{l}(t,x)=\sum_{k=1}^{l}y_{k}(t)e_{k},\quad l\geq 2,

(3.49)

which satisfies the following ordinary differential equations:

\begin{cases}\int_{\Omega}\bar{J}_{\kappa}w_{lt}\psi-\int_{\Omega}\bar{J}_{\kappa}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})^{i}_{j}q_{l}\partial_{i}\psi^{j}+\kappa\int_{\Gamma_{1}}w_{l}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\psi\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\kappa\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\bar{\partial}_{i}(w_{l}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa})\bar{\partial}_{i}(\psi\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa})\\
=\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\sigma L_{\bar{h}}\bar{\eta}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\psi\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}+\int_{\Omega}\psi H\quad\textit{$\forall\psi\in span(e_{1},...,e_{l})$, in $[0,T]$},\\
w_{l}(0)=(u_{0})_{l},\quad\textit{in $\Omega$,}\end{cases}

(3.50)

where
L_{\bar{h}}=\frac{\sqrt{\bar{h}}}{\sqrt{\bar{h}_{\kappa}}}
,
q_{l}=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\bar{A}_{\kappa})_{i}^{j}\partial_{j}w_{l}^{i}
, and
(u_{0})_{l}
denotes the
L^{2}(\Omega)
projection of
u_{0}
on
span(e_{1},...,e_{l})
. The Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem indicates the local well-posedness of
w_{l}
on time interval
[0,T_{max}]
. Choosing
\psi=w_{l}
, we obtain the energy law for
\forall t\in(0,T_{max})

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{2}\|\bar{J}_{\kappa}^{\frac{1}{2}}w_{l}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\|w_{l}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{1})}^{2}+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\|\bar{J}_{\kappa}^{\frac{1}{2}}q_{l}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\bar{J}_{\kappa}|w_{l}|^{2}\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\|(u_{0})_{l}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}L_{\bar{h}}\bar{\eta}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}w_{l}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}w_{l}H\end{aligned}

(3.51)

Followed by our assumption on
\bar{J}_{\kappa}
, we can choose a shorter time interval
t\in[0,T]
if necessary such that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\|w_{l}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\kappa\int_{0}^{t}\|w_{l}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{1})}^{2}+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\|q_{l}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C(N_{0},M_{0})\quad\textit{$\forall t\in[0,T]$}.\end{aligned}

(3.52)

For every
\psi\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\Omega))
, we substitute it into equation (
3.50
), it is straightforward to verify that the integration is well-defined with an estimate

\displaystyle\|w_{lt}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\Omega)}\leq C(N_{0},M_{0}).

(3.53)

Now, we can deduce that there exists a subsequence (still labeled as
l
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} w_{l}&\rightharpoonup^{*}w_{\varepsilon}\quad\textit{in $L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))$},\\
w_{l}&\rightharpoonup w_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in $L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))$},\\
q_{l}&\rightharpoonup q_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in $L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))$},\\
w_{l}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}&\rightharpoonup s_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in $L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma_{1}))$},\\
w_{lt}&\rightharpoonup r_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in $L^{2}(0,T;H^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\Omega))$}.\end{aligned}

(3.54)

Given
w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
, we regard
\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}
as an element of
L^{2}(0,T;(H_{0}^{1}(\Omega))^{*})
. By the definition of
q_{l}
, for any
\rho\in L_{t}^{2}H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
, we also have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}\rho&=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\bar{J_{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\bar{J}_{\kappa}^{-1}\rho)\\
&=-\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\bar{J_{\kappa}}w_{l}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\bar{J}_{\kappa}^{-1}\rho)\\
&=\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{l}\rho\\
&=-\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon}\rho\end{aligned}

(3.55)

Thus,
\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}=-\varepsilon q_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{2}(0,T;(H_{0}^{1}(\Omega))^{*})
. Since
q_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
, we have
\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
and

\displaystyle\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(w_{l})\rightharpoonup\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(w_{\varepsilon})\quad\text{in $L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))$}.

(3.56)

Similarly, for any
\rho^{\prime}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma_{1}))
, we can find
\rho^{\prime}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
(with a little abuse of notation, we choose the same symbol) such that
\rho^{\prime}
is equal to its boundary trace. Now, integrating by part, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}w_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\rho^{\prime}&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{|(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}|}+\bar{J_{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{|\bar{J}_{\kappa}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}|})\\
&=\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{l}\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{|(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}|}+\bar{J_{\kappa}}w_{l}\cdot\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{|\bar{J}_{\kappa}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}|})\\
&=\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}w_{l}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\rho^{\prime}\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}s_{\varepsilon}\rho^{\prime}\end{aligned}

(3.57)

Consequently, as
s_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma_{1}))
, we have

\displaystyle w_{l}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup w_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\quad\text{in $L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma_{1}))$ }.

(3.58)

We show
r_{\varepsilon}=w_{\varepsilon t}
in a similar fashion:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}r_{\varepsilon}\rho_{1}(t)\rho_{2}(x)&=\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}w_{lt}\rho_{1}(t)\rho_{2}(x)\quad\textit{$\forall\rho_{1}\in C^{1}_{c}(0,T)$, $\forall\rho_{2}\in H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\Omega)$}\\
&=-\lim_{l\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}w_{l}\partial_{t}\rho_{1}(t)\rho_{2}(x)\\
&=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}w_{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\rho_{1}(t)\rho_{2}(x).\end{aligned}

(3.59)

Hence,

\displaystyle w_{lt}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup r_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{in $L^{2}(0,T;H^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\Omega))$}.

(3.60)

We summarize that

\displaystyle\|w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\|\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\|w_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma_{1}))}\leq C(N_{0},M_{0}).

(3.61)

For any
\rho^{\prime\prime}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{\frac{3}{2}}(\Omega))
, the weakly limit satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\bar{J}_{\kappa}w_{\varepsilon t}\rho^{\prime\prime}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\bar{J}_{\kappa}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})^{i}_{j}q_{\varepsilon}\partial_{i}(\rho^{\prime\prime})^{j}+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}w_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\rho^{\prime\prime}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\bar{\partial}_{i}(w_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa})\bar{\partial}_{i}(\rho^{\prime\prime}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa})\\
=&\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{1}}\sigma L_{\bar{h}}\bar{\eta}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}\rho^{\prime\prime}\cdot\bar{n}_{\kappa}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\rho^{\prime\prime}H.\end{aligned}

(3.62)

Thus, we can assert that

\displaystyle w_{\varepsilon t}+\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}q_{\varepsilon}=H

(3.63)

in the sense of
L_{t}^{2}H^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\Omega)
. In other words,
(w_{\varepsilon},q_{\varepsilon})
is the weak solution to the penalized problem. Since
\nabla_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}q_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;(H_{0}^{1}(\Omega))^{*})
, we can identify the equality (
3.63
) in the same space. Since
\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;(H_{0}^{1}(\Omega))^{*})
, we have the relation

\displaystyle\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}(t)=\text{curl }u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\varepsilon^{.ij}\partial_{l}w_{\varepsilon}^{j}\partial_{t}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{l}+\int_{0}^{t}\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}H\quad\textit{in $L^{2}(0,T;(H_{0}^{1}(\Omega))^{*})$}.

(3.64)

The process of enhancing the regularity of
w_{\varepsilon}
will be segmented into the following steps: (1) enhancing interior regularity, (2) improving the regularity of
\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}
using a transport equation, (3) advancing boundary regularity through a difference quotient, and (4) combining steps (1)-(3) with estimates of
\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}
and Hodge’s elliptic estimate to obtain
w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
.

Step 1: For any smooth subdomain
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\subset\Omega
, we can pick smooth function
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
\psi=1
in
\Omega^{\prime}
. Choosing
\epsilon^{\prime}
small enough such that
\epsilon^{\prime}<dist(supp\psi,\Omega^{c})
, we denote standard Friederich’s mollifier by
\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
. For any
f\in W^{1.\infty}(\Omega)
, we investigate the equality in
H^{-1}(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*(f\partial(\psi w_{\varepsilon}))-f\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*(\partial(\psi w_{\varepsilon}))&=\int\partial\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(x-y)(f(x)-f(y))\psi w_{\varepsilon}(y)dy\\
&-\int\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(x-y)\partial f(y)\psi w_{\varepsilon}(y)dy.\end{aligned}

(3.65)

Since
w_{\varepsilon t}\in L^{2}(0,T;(H_{0}^{1}(\Omega))^{*})
and
w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
, we know (
3.65
) is well-defined for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.
The RHS of (
3.65
) can be estimated in
L^{2}(\Omega)
by mean value theorem and Young inequality:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\int\partial\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(x-y)(f(x)-f(y))\psi w_{\varepsilon}(y)dy-\int\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(x-y)\partial f(y)\psi w_{\varepsilon}(y)dy\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
\lesssim&(\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}+\|\partial\rho\|_{L^{1}})\|f\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}\|w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.\end{aligned}

(3.66)

From, (
3.64
), we can write

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}(\psi w_{\varepsilon}(t))=\psi\text{curl }u_{0}+\varepsilon^{.ij}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{l}\partial_{l}\psi w^{j}+\int_{0}^{t}\varepsilon^{.ij}\psi\partial_{l}w_{\varepsilon}^{j}\partial_{t}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{l}+\int_{0}^{t}\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}H\psi\quad\textit{in $L^{2}(0,T;(H_{0}^{1}(\Omega))^{*})$}\end{aligned}

(3.67)

Invoking (
3.66
), we can find that

\displaystyle\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*(\psi w_{\varepsilon}(t))=\psi\text{curl }u_{0}+\varepsilon^{.ij}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{l}\partial_{l}\psi w^{j}+\int_{0}^{t}\varepsilon^{.ij}\psi(\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*\partial_{l}w_{\varepsilon}^{j})\partial_{t}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{l}+\mathcal{R},

(3.68)

where
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim C(N_{0},M_{0})
.
The divergence type estimate can be derived in a similar strategy:

\displaystyle\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{R},

(3.69)

where
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim C(N_{0},M_{0})
.
By virtue of (
2.27
), we can show that

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*(\psi w_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C(N_{0},M_{0})+C(N_{0},M_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\|\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*(\psi w_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\quad\textit{for a.e. $t\in(0,T)$}.

(3.70)

Thus, for a
T
small enough, we find the estimate independent on
\varepsilon^{\prime}
that

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*(\psi w_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))}\leq C(N_{0},M_{0}).

(3.71)

It implies that
\psi w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
and
w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega^{\prime}))
.

Remark
.

In this instance, a modified type of the elliptic estimate (
2.27
) is employed. Readers can readily derive an appropriate version. Alternatively, we can utilize
(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{j}-\delta_{i}^{j}=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{j}
and introduce a small
T
to arrive at a similar conclusion.

Let
\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
be the horizontal mollifier. We claim
\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
. By definition of the horizontal mollifier, we have

\displaystyle\|\bar{\partial}\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{y\})}\lesssim_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\|w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{y\})}.

(3.72)

Integrating with respect to the variable
y
, we have

\displaystyle\|\bar{\partial}\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\|w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(3.73)

Now, similar to
[
10
]
, we use
\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}
,
\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}
and
\bar{\partial}w_{\varepsilon}
to express
\partial_{3}w_{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle(\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon},\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}^{1}w_{\varepsilon},\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}^{2}w_{\varepsilon})^{T}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}M_{i}^{\kappa}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon},

(3.74)

where

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &M_{0}^{\kappa}=\begin{bmatrix}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{1}^{1}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{2}^{1}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{1}\\
0&-(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{1}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{2}^{1}\\
(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{1}&0&-(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{1}^{1}\end{bmatrix}\quad M_{2}^{\kappa}=\begin{bmatrix}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{1}^{2}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{2}^{2}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{2}\\
0&-(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{2}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{2}^{2}\\
(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{2}&0&-(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{1}^{2}\end{bmatrix}\\
&M_{3}^{\kappa}=\begin{bmatrix}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{1}^{3}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{2}^{3}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{3}\\
0&-(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{3}&(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{2}^{3}\\
(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{3}&0&-(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{1}^{3}.\end{bmatrix}\end{aligned}

(3.75)

A direct computation yields that

\displaystyle\det M_{3}^{\kappa}=(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{3}^{3}\sum_{i=1}^{3}|(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}|^{2}.

(3.76)

Thus,
M_{3}^{\kappa}
is invertible. In brief, we express

\displaystyle\partial_{3}w_{\varepsilon}=\tilde{M}\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}+\tilde{N}\text{div }_{{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}}w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{L}_{i}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon},

(3.77)

where
\tilde{M}
,
\tilde{N}
, and
\tilde{L}_{i}
denote matrices with entries consist of
\bar{A}^{\kappa}
.

From (
3.63
), it indicates that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}(\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon^{.ij}\partial_{t}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{l}\partial_{l}(w_{\varepsilon})_{j}=\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}H,\\
&\partial_{t}(\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon^{.ij}\partial_{t}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}(\tilde{M}\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}+\tilde{N}\text{div }_{{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}}w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{L}_{i}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon})_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{2}\varepsilon^{.ij}\partial_{t}(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{l}\partial_{l}(w_{\varepsilon})_{j}+\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}H.\end{aligned}

(3.78)

In short, we can rewrite it as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon})+\tilde{M}^{\prime}\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}=\tilde{N}^{\prime}\text{div }_{{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}}w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{L}_{i}^{\prime}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon}+\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}H,

(3.79)

where
\tilde{M}^{\prime}
,
\tilde{N}^{\prime}
, and
\tilde{L}_{i}^{\prime}
are matrices in lower order. The transport equation can be solved as

\displaystyle\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}w_{\varepsilon}=\tilde{P}^{-1}\text{curl }u_{0}+\tilde{P}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{P}(\tilde{N}^{\prime}\text{div }_{{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}}w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{L}_{i}^{\prime}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon}+\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}H),

(3.80)

with some matrix
\tilde{P}
. Plunge it back to (
3.77
):

\displaystyle\partial_{3}w_{\varepsilon}=\tilde{M}\tilde{P}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{P}\tilde{L}_{i}^{\prime}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{L}_{i}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{R},

(3.81)

where
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim C(N_{0},M_{0})
.

Remark
.

In the absence of a direct estimate for the commutator of the horizontal mollifier and
\partial_{3}
, we are required to represent
\partial_{3}
as
\bar{\partial}
and lower-order terms. The transport equation of “vorticity” and the constraint of “divergence-free” enable this strategy to be applicable for “velocity”. However, we mention that the relation (
3.77
) is true for any function.

Now, we horizontally mollify (
3.81
), it leads to the desired estimate

\displaystyle\|\partial_{3}\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq_{\epsilon^{\prime}}C(N_{0},M_{0}).

(3.82)

Hence, we arrive at

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))}\leq_{\epsilon^{\prime}}C(N_{0},M_{0}).

(3.83)

Remark
.

Up to this stage, we have already established the
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega))
regularity of the mollified equation. However, it would be more convenient to use an elliptic estimate to form higher regularity in the following. Thus, we need to control the commutator of
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
and
\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}
,
\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}
.

Thanks to the relation (
3.81
), we have the estimate of commutator:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*((\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}\partial_{3}w_{\varepsilon})-(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*\partial_{3}w_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}\\
\lesssim&\|[\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*,(\bar{A}^{\kappa})_{i}^{3}](\tilde{M}\tilde{P}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{P}\tilde{L}_{i}^{\prime}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{L}_{i}\partial_{i}w_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\mathcal{R}\\
\leq&C(N_{0},M_{0}).\end{aligned}

(3.84)

It implies that
\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}
and
\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}
commute effectively with the horizontal mollifier
\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, given controlled by a constant
C(N_{0},M_{0})
. We express
\text{curl }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}
and
\text{div }_{\bar{A}^{\kappa}}\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}
into the combination of
\bar{\partial}\Lambda_{\epsilon^{\prime}}*w_{\varepsilon}
and lower order term. If we can express
\partial_{3}w_{\varepsilon}
into such forms, we can always achieve this. In our case, the domain is very simple. On
\{x_{3}=0\}
, the normal trace is always zero. On
\{x_{3}=1\}
, we apply normal trace estimate and commutation type lemma (
2.21
) to obtain desired estimate. This scheme always holds for the higher regularity improvement. After this, we will use a Grönwall type inequality to obtain the uniform estimate.

Next, we apply horizontal mollification to the function.
